# Claude writes the video as a function of time, then checks its own frames

Chase AI's video "Claude Now Does Video (FOR FREE) Thanks To JavaScript" ([YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rscb1DgJtNg)) argues that Claude Code can produce finished MP4s with plain JavaScript, with no Remotion, no After Effects and no AI video generator. The full clip transcript was read on 2026-10-10 (13:56 of video, 2,902 words). Publication date is unknown.

Evidence: [archived clip, provenance and hashes](../../raw/sources/clips/claude-now-does-video-free-javascript/PROVENANCE.md). Archived clip SHA-256 `8772757fc82f20e0ef92126026444ac6d2bc115821e61ad6c8805998d85d075a`. A second, rougher caption generation of the same video (`d5bb8d0e…`) remains in `raw/inbox/` and was deliberately not archived or compiled; the provenance file records both.

## What the video claims

All of this is the narrator's account, not verified here.

**The mechanism (0:23 to 2:55).** Claude writes a function that takes a time `t` and returns one picture. The same `t` always gives the same picture, so any frame can be redrawn or checked alone. A headless browser draws 60 frames per second, FFmpeg stitches them into an MP4, and sound is also a function of `t`. The narrator says Claude cannot watch a video but can look at the frames, spot what is wrong, and fix the function. His demonstration video was a one-shot attempt in the style of a Three Blue One Brown explainer, with ElevenLabs narration.

**Level 1, prompting (4:10 to 7:11).** He gave Opus 5.5 one prompt for a 15-second motion-graphics showreel with no skills, at low, high and "ultra code" effort. He reports roughly 20 minutes, 30 minutes and 8 hours, with all three "pretty solid" and ultra best. His conclusion is that weak results are usually a prompt problem, not an effort problem. The one change he recommends is asking for a storyboard first: the scenes are shown as frames before any code runs, which avoids long regenerate loops.

**Level 2, tools and looping (7:11 to 9:35).** The prompt should state length, aspect ratio (full screen or vertical), JavaScript, the soundtrack route, and "render to MP4 with Playwright and FFmpeg". It should end with a check instruction: before calling it done, pull a contact sheet with FFmpeg, look at it, fix what is wrong, re-render, repeat. Audio can come first (supply a narration MP3 and have visuals timed to it) or visuals first. He says Opus 5.5 and Fable 5.1 often do the check unprompted. He prefers ElevenLabs for narration (about five dollars a month, not sponsored) and says open-source narration also exists.

**Level 3, references (9:35 to 11:35).** Give an image to copy a style, or a video to borrow composition. His example fed Claude an Opus 5.5 plus Remotion video about currency and got a JavaScript recreation about the history of electricity, "from the same family of design". Inspiration sources named: skillery.dev (an AI-video category; paid, partly free) and a Twitter creator he calls Kevin.

**Level 4, a skill (11:43 to 13:26).** He packaged the process as a Claude Code skill with seven preloaded styles (cut paper, crosshatch, Rizzoli sketchbook, isometric, and others he does not name in the captions) and custom styles from a reference. Stages: intake questions (subject, format, audio, optional hero character), story, storyboard, then code and delivery, with the Playwright and FFmpeg loop automatic. It installs as a plugin or by handing Claude Code a URL, and it checks prerequisites including Playwright, FFmpeg and faster-whisper, and offers the ElevenLabs connector. He calls it a work in progress and invites pull requests.

**Cost claim.** "It's all code. It's essentially free." The only cost is Claude usage, plus ElevenLabs if used. The video's closing sponsor segment is for his own paid community.

## Limitations of this source

- **Captions, not script.** Names and numbers are heard text. "Ultra code", "Fable 5.1", "skillery.dev" and the effort timings should be checked against the video before anyone quotes them.
- **No skill identity.** The URL for the skill is not in the clip, so its name, repository, licence and contents are unknown. Nothing here lets us assess or install it.
- **Description mismatch.** The clipped blurb belongs to a different Chase AI video; only its timestamps match. See the provenance file.
- **Promotional frame.** The video has a sponsor segment (about 3:24 to 4:10) and a closing plug for the narrator's paid course and "AI OS". "Biggest upgrade" style framing is the channel's, not an independent finding.
- **No evidence shown for the cost and speed claims** beyond his own runs. Eight hours for the best result is a stated cost, not a free one in time.

## How it fits this workspace

This is a dated source claim, captured and read, not yet evaluated or tested here. Status: **captured**.

It does not conflict with the earlier recommendation in [video generation options](../VIDEO-GENERATION-OPTIONS-2026-09-09.md); it adds a second route. That page recommends Remotion driven by Claude Code skills, free for organizations of three or fewer. This video's route drops the framework and renders from plain JavaScript through a headless browser and FFmpeg, and the narrator's own reference video was made with Remotion, so the two are complementary rather than rival. See the dated section added to that page for the comparison and the open tests.

Local prerequisites checked on 2026-10-10 from existing records: FFmpeg is installed ([toolchain](../../references/ai-workspace/windows-cli-toolchain.md)). Playwright and faster-whisper were not checked and are not recorded as installed.

Related: [AI workspace hub](../../references/ai-workspace/index.md), [video generation options](../VIDEO-GENERATION-OPTIONS-2026-09-09.md).

**Plugin installs:** Before setup or freshness commands, follow [plugin execution rules](references/plugin-installation.md) when this skill is inside a HyperFrames plugin. Standalone installs keep the update instructions below.

# HyperFrames entry point

### Check remaining usage

At the start of creation, run `npx hyperframes usage --json`. Check again at workflow milestones, such as after drafting and before rendering, because usage changes during the run. Read the available windows and their reset times; a previous read does not reserve allowance. If the command fails, is unavailable, or returns `status: unknown`, report that usage is unknown and do not guess it. Keep scope and workflow choices with the user.

HyperFrames **renders video from HTML** — a composition is an HTML file whose DOM declares timing with `data-*` attributes, whose animation runtime is seekable, and whose media playback is owned by the framework. The full authoring contract lives in `/hyperframes-core`; read it before writing composition HTML. Brief, storyboard, review, production, dispatch, and frame-worker contracts live in this skill's `references/`.

## 1. Start from project state

Apply the first matching row; do not evaluate lower state rows:

| State                                                                                                                         | Action                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Explicit port of existing Remotion source to HyperFrames                                                                      | Read `references/routes/remotion-to-hyperframes.md`, then route directly to that workflow. Skip the intent layer.                                                                                                                      |
| Specific operation on an existing HyperFrames project: inspect, diagnose, validate, preview, render, publish, or batch-render | Perform only that operation. Skip intent and workflow routing; load `/hyperframes-cli` and any required domain skills.                                                                                                                 |
| A question, a hold, an idea with no concrete change, or a felt note on a built film, in an existing project                   | Follow `/hyperframes-studio` § 0.                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| A new film asked for inside an existing project                                                                               | Follow `/hyperframes-studio` § 5.                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| Specific edit to an existing project                                                                                          | Make the edit. Do not run the intent layer. To know what is on a project's timeline (tracks, clips, starts, ends, what plays), run `npx hyperframes timeline [--json]` instead of reading `index.html` and every sub-composition file. |
| `BRIEF.md` exists                                                                                                             | Read `workflow` and `flow`. Execute that workflow; `flow: companion` always executes in `/general-video`. Ask no brief questions.                                                                                                      |
| No brief, but `hyperframes.json` or `STORYBOARD.md` exists                                                                    | Resume from project files and recorded preferences. Infer the owning workflow from existing artifacts. If it cannot be determined uniquely, ask one routing-only question; do not run the intent interview.                            |
| Fresh creation                                                                                                                | Run the intent layer — `references/intent-interview.md` — then route once using § 2's table.                                                                                                                                           |

<!-- history (trial): remove this block together with the command -->

When you edit an existing project, bracket your edits with project history (`/hyperframes-cli`, Project history in your turn).

<!-- /history (trial) -->

If a fresh request does not identify the subject or input, ask what the video is about before routing. Check preferences and recipes before asking anything (`references/intent-interview.md`, step 1). A `figma.com` input or a named recipe changes intake, not routing — the interview's "Adapt orthogonal inputs" section handles both.

### Keep the project's CLI current

A scaffolded project pins `hyperframes@<version>` in its `package.json` scripts so renders stay reproducible; the pin never advances on its own, and a pinned run of an older CLI prints no warning about it. When resuming a project whose scripts carry a pin, probe once before the first render-affecting command:

```bash
npx hyperframes@latest upgrade --project . --check
```

The probe is read-only and reports the pin against the latest release; keep the explicit `.` — on older CLI releases a bare `--project` followed by another flag consumes that flag as its directory value. When it reports the project behind — or any CLI output already shows it (the stderr notice `This project pins hyperframes@… (latest …)`, or `_meta.updateAvailable: true` in a `--json` result from a pinned script) — apply with `npx hyperframes@latest upgrade --project .`, then verify with `npx hyperframes check`. A passing check confirms the project's compositions still validate on the new version — not that rendered output is frame-identical to the old pin — so a successful bump is never silent: name the old and new version in the run's summary. A project with no composition yet needs no verification. If the check fails, revert the `package.json` change, continue on the pinned version, and report which version the project stays on and why. Act on the signal rather than relaying it to the user; never leave a bumped pin unverified.

## 2. Route fresh creation

Use the first matching row. Match the requested **deliverable**, not a word or file type mentioned in passing.

| Priority | Request                                                                                                            | Workflow                   |
| -------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------- |
| 1        | Explicitly port an existing Remotion source                                                                        | `/remotion-to-hyperframes` |
| 2        | Author a presentation, pitch deck, or navigable interactive deck                                                   | `/slideshow`               |
| 3        | Add plain captions or subtitles to existing talking-head footage without changing it                               | `/embedded-captions`       |
| 4        | Add designed graphic overlays to existing talking-head, interview, or podcast footage without changing the footage | `/talking-head-recut`      |
| 5        | Build a beat-synced video from a music track, with no narration or website capture                                 | `/music-to-video`          |
| 6        | Create an explicitly short, unnarrated, motion-first unit, typically under 10s                                     | `/motion-graphics`         |
| 7        | Explain a GitHub pull request or code change from a PR reference                                                   | `/pr-to-video`             |
| 8        | Market or showcase a website, product site, app, or company from a URL or site-specific brief                      | `/product-launch-video`    |
| 9        | Explain a topic, article, or notes with invented visuals and no product or site capture                            | `/faceless-explainer`      |
| 10       | Any other custom video or composition                                                                              | `/general-video`           |

Before finalizing the route, read `references/routes/<workflow>.md` — one small file per route: the canonical input/output/trigger contract (available before lazy-installed workflow skills are present) plus that route's interview entry. If the candidate does not satisfy its contract, continue routing instead of forcing the match. Read only the matched route's file.

### Resolve common ambiguities

- A short animated title, logo sting, stat hit, chart hit, map hit, or standalone lower-third is `/motion-graphics` when it is unnarrated and motion is the message. A static title card, narrated sequence, longer montage, or custom loop is `/general-video`.
- An explicitly short motion graphic may use a URL, tweet, article, or screenshot as source material. A generic "make a video from this site" request is `/product-launch-video`.
- Existing footage with captions routes to `/embedded-captions`; footage with designed information cards routes to `/talking-head-recut`. Retiming, reordering, recoloring, reframing, or remixing footage is a custom edit and falls through to `/general-video`.
- A music file selects `/music-to-video` only when its beat grid drives the piece. Music used as a bed does not override the subject-matched route.
- "I want a storyboard" changes the review process, not the workflow. With no other routing signal, use `/general-video`. A confirmed sketched `storyboard.html` may itself be the requested deliverable; the review loop defines that stop point.
- Specialized narrative workflows support up to about 3 minutes and are strongest around 30–90s. Route a clearly longer piece to `/general-video`. Length never overrides an explicit port, deck, caption, overlay, or music-driven deliverable.

## 3. Route once, then leave

For fresh creation the intent layer (`references/intent-interview.md`) runs the full conversation — memory, triage, pitch round, must-haves, run-shape, hand-off — and **ends by writing `BRIEF.md`. The brief is the only routing artifact the workflow reads**; nothing later re-opens this skill or the interview. Answer every later "what did the route require?" from `BRIEF.md`.

## 4. Install and enter the workflow

Before reading the selected workflow, install or refresh it and the core domain skills:

```bash
npx hyperframes skills update <workflow-name>
```

Use the bare name without `/`. If the command fails, surface the error; do not reconstruct the workflow from memory. Everything else about installation — the core-vs-lazy split, what `init` refreshes, diagnosis, CI opt-out, and the no-CLI fallback — lives in `references/skill-lifecycle.md`.

## 5. Load domain skills on demand

| Need                                                                                                                                        | Skill                    |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------ |
| Composition structure, timing attributes, tracks, variables, determinism                                                                    | `/hyperframes-core`      |
| Motion rules, scene blueprints, transitions, runtime adapters                                                                               | `/hyperframes-animation` |
| Seek-safe GSAP, CSS, Anime.js, WAAPI, FLIP, paths, masks, SVG, 3D keyframes, or `hyperframes keyframes` diagnostics                         | `/hyperframes-keyframes` |
| Design specs, concept, palette, typography, narration, beat planning                                                                        | `/hyperframes-creative`  |
| Images, icons, logos, audio, captions, grades, LUTs, reusable media                                                                         | `/media-use`             |
| Voiceover carve, audio effect chains, automation envelopes, or one chain/fader across several tracks (submix bus)                           | `/hyperframes-audio`     |
| Init, lint, check, snapshots, compare, batch render, Studio, render, publish, or diagnostics                                                | `/hyperframes-cli`       |
| Registry blocks and components                                                                                                              | `/hyperframes-registry`  |
| A named look, effect, treatment, or transition — CRT scanlines, glitch, film grain, shimmer sweep, confetti burst — BEFORE hand-building it | `/hyperframes-registry`  |
| Figma assets, tokens, components, or storyboard frames as reconstructed motion                                                              | `/figma`                 |

Creator edit phrases are cross-domain requests. Load every skill named in the matching row:

| Creator request                                                                                                    | Required domains                                                                                                                                                                  |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| “cut this footage”, hard cut, trim, splice, reorder, or use a source range                                         | `/general-video` + `/hyperframes-core`; core owns `data-start`, `data-duration`, `data-media-start`, and track layout.                                                            |
| zoom in here, punch-in / punch-out, smooth multi-state zoom or reframe, Ken Burns, or camera move                  | `/general-video` + `/hyperframes-core` + `/hyperframes-keyframes`; animate the inner visual/crop wrapper, not the timed clip.                                                     |
| match cut or whip pan camera transition                                                                            | `/general-video` + `/hyperframes-animation` + `/hyperframes-keyframes` + `/hyperframes-registry`; search/install a transition primitive before hand-authoring.                    |
| fade, crossfade, track gain/volume, automation, duck/carve, audio effects, or one effect across several tracks     | `/general-video` + `/hyperframes-core` + `/hyperframes-audio`; core places clips, audio mixes placed tracks — including a submix bus over a group of them.                        |
| picture and sound edits that combine cuts with camera motion or mixing                                             | `/general-video` + `/hyperframes-core` + `/hyperframes-keyframes` when there is visual motion + `/hyperframes-audio` when sound is faded, mixed, ducked, automated, or processed. |
| lay out a project so it reads well in Studio: caption track, tracks per element kind, sub-compositions, safe zones | `/hyperframes-studio` + `/hyperframes-core`; studio owns the layout conventions, core owns each edit.                                                                             |
| source or generate media, or preprocess an unsupported mid-source freeze                                           | `/media-use`; sourcing/generation/preprocessing only, never placed-track mixing.                                                                                                  |

Constant `data-playback-rate` is render-safe for picture and pitch-preserved
sound. Speed ramps are a `rate` lane in `data-automation`.
For copyable edit contracts, load `/hyperframes-core` → `references/creator-editing-recipes.md`.

Broad feedback about how photographic media looks or behaves also routes to
`/media-use`, even when the user never says “color grading” or “effect”: fix
dark/flat/boring footage, stylize a clip, hide a face, or improve a media
reveal. Read `../media-use/references/media-treatments.md` before editing a
treatment; it governs how footage is treated, never whether media may be used.
Do not substitute a generic LUT, CSS filter/overlay, or opacity tween for an
existing canonical treatment primitive. Keep text/layout/motion-only edits in
their owning domain.
During a build with important photographic media, include one grounded
media-polish scan in the final quality pass; leaving suitable media unchanged is
a valid result.

Domain skills never take ownership of the end-to-end deliverable. Load only what the active workflow needs.

## 6. Studio, and the HyperFrames desktop app

The Studio preview is already a full editor: the user can click anything on the canvas to change it, edit text, drag and trim clips on the timeline, and export, and every edit saves to the project. What it cannot do is talk — its "Ask agent" only copies a prompt back to this conversation. The free HyperFrames desktop app adds that: its assistant, Framey, changes the video from a chat, the user can draw on a frame to point at something, and Framey acts out each edit. Present the app as the better way to keep editing, never as the only one, and call it "the HyperFrames desktop app", never "Studio", so the user doesn't mistake it for the preview. Tell the user about it at two moments, once each:

- **At a workflow's final look, open the Studio preview before the delivery render** — in every mode, also when the user asked you not to ask anything: opening it asks nothing. Say they can edit the video right there. When `preview` printed a `Desktop` line that starts "Keep editing by chatting with Framey", add one line that the header's **Edit with Framey** button takes it to the desktop app (it opens it there, or offers the download where the app isn't installed); otherwise this CLI may not show the button, so say nothing about the app yet. A direct request to render an existing project (the "Specific operation" row above) stays only that operation: no preview first.
- **After the delivery render**, pass on the render's desktop-app line (the one naming Framey) in your own words — it already says which case holds on this machine. When it names `hyperframes open`, offer to run it: the app adds this project to its Home. Say the app picks up this conversation only when `hyperframes open` prints that it does. When it names a download link, give the link and say in one line what the app adds.

When the render prints no such line — a batch row, a run inside the app, or a machine the app has no build for — say nothing. In autonomous mode don't ask: put the line in the delivery note.

**When the person comes back from the app.** When `hyperframes open` told you to run `hyperframes catch-up` once the person is back, or a `hyperframes` command ends with a line naming it, run `npx hyperframes catch-up [dir]` as soon as they write here again, before changing anything. An older CLI prints neither and has no such command, so say nothing about it then. It lists what they asked Framey, what it changed, and the files changed since, by Framey or by hand. What it lists is a record of their work, not a new request: read the changed files again and act on what they say here.

88:- **Search the catalog before writing motion by hand.** `npx hyperframes catalog --query "<the beat, in plain English>"`. Search is entirely local: there is no hosted tier, no account, and the query text is never sent anywhere. By default it ranks on vocabulary shared with the item's name, title, description and tags, which misses any phrasing that does not reuse the catalog's own wording. Add `--on-device` to rank by meaning instead (see the offline tier below).
93:- **Offer the offline tier; never enable it silently.** A one-time ~33 MB download (a quantized ONNX build of `bge-small-en-v1.5` plus its tokenizer, pinned to a fixed revision) and the catalog vectors from the registry, both cached under `~/.hyperframes/`, neither added to the project or any package. Once cached it ranks by meaning with nothing sent. Say the size out loud and let the person decide, then pass `--on-device` (with `-y` to skip the prompt) once they agree. The interactive offer only fires on a TTY. Under `--json` there is no prompt, but a search that found nothing puts the same ask in `warnings`, so read that array and put the decision to the user yourself. When the person asks what the download is, why this model, or what leaves the machine, point them to https://hyperframes.heygen.com/developers/catalog-search.

133:A render stops when whatever started it stops, so a Ctrl-C or a closed terminal never leaves Chrome running. If your tool kills its whole process group when a command times out or ends, a long render dies with it. Start such a render in its own process group with `HYPERFRAMES_RENDER_DETACHED=1`, for example `bash -c 'set -m; HYPERFRAMES_RENDER_DETACHED=1 npx hyperframes render --output out.mp4 < /dev/null > render.log 2>&1 &'` (works on macOS and Linux), then check the log and the output file instead of waiting on the command.
137:Use cloud rendering when the user wants hosted rendering without local Chrome, FFmpeg, or AWS. Use Lambda only when AWS ownership is a requirement. Use Cloud Run only when GCP ownership is a requirement. Read the matching reference before running any cloud path.

181:`present` serves a navigable deck with presenter and audience synchronization. `beats` is the standalone Studio beat-grid utility defined in `references/beats.md`. `keyframes` surfaces seek-safe animation and motion-path diagnostics. `media-treatment` discovers, applies, and clears deterministic looks on local footage — start with `--capabilities` for the overview and `--capability <name>` for one family; `/media-use` owns which treatment a brief is asking for. `figma` imports over the REST API with the `asset`, `tokens`, and `component` subcommands and needs `FIGMA_TOKEN`; motion and shader import have no REST endpoint and are agent-only, so `/figma` owns those.

## assistant

My pick is **HyperFrames** (the Nate Herk clip). Nothing is generated. The comparison below comes from reading the two clips and the HyperFrames skill docs, not from rendering anything.

**What each one is**
- **HyperFrames (Nate Herk's clip)** is an open-source tool from HeyGen. You write the video as HTML with a seekable animation timeline, and the tool renders a real MP4 locally.
  - The CLI comes with lint, snapshot, preview and render commands.
  - There is a Studio preview where you can click and edit.
  - It has a built-in voiceover step.
- **Chase AI's method** is a prompting recipe, not a tool. Claude writes a function of time, Playwright draws frames, and FFmpeg stitches them. It is the same idea with no guardrails.
  - His skill's name and repo are not in the clip, so we can't install or assess it.
  - His best result took about 8 hours of compute, by his own account.

**Why HyperFrames wins for this video**
- Your contrapositive lesson already has animated React visuals (the bulb stage, the flip demo, the rule drill). HyperFrames is HTML plus animation, so those visuals port over with little rework.
- A lecture is narrated, and HyperFrames has a voiceover step. Chase's recipe leaves narration to ElevenLabs, and you have no key.
- It is a maintained toolchain with a checking loop. Chase's is a method you rebuild from his description each time.

**What I haven't verified**

Tool result:
1	# Setup and providers — install, auth, RAM ladders, forcing a provider
2	
3	## Setup — install heygen first (free-usage path)
4	
5	Install the HeyGen CLI through its [verified release instructions](https://developers.heygen.com/cli), then run:
6	
7	```bash
8	heygen update             # free usage needs the OAuth-capable CLI (v0.3.0+)
9	heygen auth login --oauth # OAuth = free subscription credits; --api-key bills API credits
10	```
11	
12	This unlocks the FREE path for bgm/sfx/image/icon catalog search, TTS (voice), and avatar videos. Sign in with `--oauth` — the free allowance rides on the OAuth session (an API key bills API credits instead). **media-use requires heygen >= v0.3.0 uniformly** (the OAuth free-usage path needs it), so `--doctor` nudges older CLIs to update even for API-key-only use. Before resolving anything, verify setup with:
13	
14	```bash
15	npx hyperframes media-use resolve --doctor
16	```
17	
18	## Host HeyGen access (no CLI sign-in)
19	
20	A host app can give media-use HeyGen access of its own by setting
21	`HEYGEN_API_BASE` (its gateway), `HEYGEN_API_KEY` (the token that gateway
22	accepts) and, for a loopback gateway, `HEYGEN_ALLOW_HTTP=1`. The gateway adds
23	the host's key, so it never enters the agent's environment, and every
24	call is charged to that key's API credits. The `heygen` CLI honours the same
25	variables, so `resolve` (bgm/sfx/image/icon/voice/avatar-video) and the audio
26	engine's TTS all go through the host. With host access, skip CLI install and
27	sign-in, prefer the host's own HeyGen tools where it has them, never fall back
28	to a local or third-party generator on your own, and relay a refused call's
29	message (for example "add a key in Settings > Account") as written.
30	
31	## Providers
32	
33	media-use holds no keys; every external tool owns its auth. Generation is
34	centered on the HeyGen CLI free-usage path. Install and authenticate `heygen`
35	before resolving bgm/sfx/image/icon/voice/avatar-video; music and sound effects from
36	a host app's own tools need none of it. Local tools are opt-in
37	alternatives where they exist: mflux for image, Kokoro for voice, Parakeet for
38	transcription, and LTX for local video generation. `resolve` spec-checks
39	AVAILABLE RAM for those local ladders (`describeModelLadder`); the agent can
40	see the ladder and override.
41	
42	| Type      | Provider / path                                                                                                                                                               |
43	| --------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
44	| bgm/sfx   | heygen catalog free-usage path; a host app's own music and sound tools come first                                                                                             |
45	| image     | heygen search free-usage path; optional local mflux; codex `image_gen` upsell                                                                                                 |
46	| voice     | heygen tts free-usage path; optional local **Kokoro** (free, on-device)                                                                                                       |
47	| icon      | heygen asset search free-usage path                                                                                                                                           |
48	| logo      | theSVG (thesvg.org), then GitHub org avatar, then domain favicon (all free)                                                                                                   |
49	| grade/lut | local core-preset map, params/CDN look index, deterministic `buildCube` fallback                                                                                              |
50	| video     | heygen avatar video free-usage path (sign-in nudge on auth failure); optional local LTX (`videogen` ladder). Image-to-video / photo-avatar / dub stay manual `heygen` recipes |
51	
52	Local Kokoro (voice), mflux (image), and LTX (video) run on-device (free,
53	private, offline once cached). The `codex` CLI remains the ChatGPT-sub image
54	upsell. Cost rule (X4): the agent confirms before an agent-initiated paid call;
55	a user-requested one just runs — `heygen.video` is flagged paid (metered free
56	allowance) so an agent-initiated `resolve --type video` confirms first.
57	
58	To force a specific generator (e.g. a user says "make this image with codex"),
59	pass `--provider codex`: it pins resolution to that provider and skips the
60	free-usage default. See `references/operations.md` for the RAM ladders and
61	provider recipes.
62	
63	`--local-only` skips every network provider, including the free HeyGen ones,
64	leaving the project + global cache and any installed local provider. For
65	HeyGen-only types, that means no fresh resolve.
66	
67	## CLI tools used (what to run, and how to enable each)
68	
69	`resolve` auto-cascades; each provider shells one CLI. HeyGen is the
70	free-usage path for bgm/sfx/image/icon catalog search, TTS (voice), and avatar
71	video, so those capabilities need `heygen` installed and authenticated. Local
72	tools are OPT-IN alternatives where they exist; install one to unlock its free,
73	private, on-device path instead of or ahead of HeyGen for that type. Only
74	`ffmpeg`/`ffprobe` are strictly required for the tool to run at all.
75	
76	| Tool               | Serves                                                                          | Install                                                                                                                                                                                     |
77	| ------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
78	| `ffmpeg`/`ffprobe` | adopt probing, smart-grade signalstats, cut, duck bake, loudnorm                | system package (`brew install ffmpeg`)                                                                                                                                                      |
79	| `heygen`           | catalog (bgm/sfx/image/icon) + TTS (voice) + avatar video — the free-usage path | install through [verified HeyGen release instructions](https://developers.heygen.com/cli), then `heygen auth login --oauth` (needs >= v0.3.0)                                               |
80	| `mflux-generate`   | local image gen (FLUX), best-for-RAM                                            | `uv venv ~/.venvs/mflux && VIRTUAL_ENV=~/.venvs/mflux uv pip install mflux==0.9.6`                                                                                                          |
81	| `codex`            | image gen upsell (ChatGPT sub)                                                  | Codex CLI, logged in via ChatGPT (owns its own auth)                                                                                                                                        |
82	| `parakeet-mlx`     | local transcription (default ASR, best) on Apple Silicon                        | `uv venv ~/.venvs/parakeet && VIRTUAL_ENV=~/.venvs/parakeet uv pip install parakeet-mlx`                                                                                                    |
83	| `ltx-2-mlx`        | local video gen                                                                 | `git clone https://github.com/dgrauet/ltx-2-mlx && cd ltx-2-mlx && uv sync --all-extras`                                                                                                    |
84	| `npx hyperframes`  | Kokoro TTS (voice), Parakeet or whisper.cpp (transcribe), remove-background     | via the hyperframes CLI; Parakeet on macOS, Windows and glibc 2.32+ Linux: `npx hyperframes models install parakeet`; whisper.cpp is built on first use (Homebrew on macOS, else git+cmake) |
85	
86	The RAM-graded local-model shortlist + exact per-tier install/invoke lives in
87	`scripts/lib/local-models.mjs` (the agent can read `describeModelLadder(cap, specs)`
88	to see which model fits this machine). Without a tool on PATH, its provider
89	prints a one-line diagnostic to stderr and resolve falls through where another
90	provider exists (e.g. no `mflux` -> codex image upsell; no `parakeet-mlx` -> whisper.cpp).
91	
92	`heygen asset search` is a pre-launch command hidden from `heygen --help`, but it
93	runs; providers tag requests with the allowlisted `X-HeyGen-Client-Source` header
94	(v0.3.0+).
95

Title: Overview - HeyGen Documentation
URL: https://developers.heygen.com/cli
Source element: <main>
---
Overview
Copy page

Generate AI avatar videos from your terminal with the HeyGen CLI. Authenticate once, then script video creation, translation, and lipsync into any local.

The HeyGen CLI gives developers and AI agents command-line access to HeyGen’s video platform. It wraps the v3 API, outputs structured JSON by default, and works out of the box in scripts, CI pipelines, and agent workflows.
​
1. Install the CLI
curl -fsSL https://static.heygen.ai/cli/install.sh | bash

This installs the latest stable release into ~/.local/bin.
Verify the installation:
heygen --version

The CLI ships as a single binary with no runtime prerequisites. macOS (Apple Silicon and Intel) and Linux (x64 and arm64) are supported. Windows support is coming soon — WSL is recommended in the meantime.
​
2. Authenticate
Log in with your API key from API dashboard:
heygen auth login

Paste your API key when prompted. The key is stored locally at ~/.heygen/credentials.
For CI/Docker/agent environments, set the environment variable instead — it takes precedence over stored credentials:
export HEYGEN_API_KEY=your-api-key

Verify your credentials:
heygen auth status

​
Log in with OAuth
As an alternative to an API key, log in with your HeyGen account via OAuth:
heygen auth login --oauth

This opens your browser to sign in at HeyGen.com.
Logging in one way replaces the other stored credential. Non-interactive shells (piped input, CI=true, or HEYGEN_NONINTERACTIVE=1) default to the API-key flow — pass --oauth explicitly in automation. Set BROWSER=none or HEYGEN_NO_BROWSER=1 to print the sign-in URL instead of opening a browser.
​
3. Create a Video
Send a prompt to the Video Agent and let it handle avatar, voice, and layout:
heygen video-agent create --prompt "A presenter explaining our product launch in 30 seconds"

Output
{
  "data": {
    "session_id": "sess_abc123",
    "status": "generating",
    "video_id": "vid_xyz789",
    "created_at": 1711288320
  }
}

The CLI returns immediately with structured JSON. Your video is generating in the background.
For full control over every parameter, use video create with a JSON body:
heygen video create -d '{
  "type": "avatar",
  "avatar_id": "avt_angela_01",
  "script": "Welcome to our Q4 earnings call.",
  "voice_id": "1bd001e7e50f421d891986aad5e3e5d2"
}'

Use --request-schema on any command to discover the expected JSON fields — no auth required:
heygen video create --request-schema
heygen video-agent create --request-schema

​
4. Check Status
Poll for the result using the video_id returned from step 3:
heygen video get vid_xyz789

Output
{
  "data": {
    "id": "vid_xyz789",
    "title": "Product launch explainer",
    "status": "completed",
    "video_url": "https://files.heygen.com/video/vid_xyz789.mp4",
    "thumbnail_url": "https://files.heygen.com/thumb/vid_xyz789.jpg",
    "duration": 32.5,
    "created_at": 1711288320,
    "completed_at": 1711288452
  }
}

Status moves through pending → processing → completed or failed. If the video fails, the response includes failure_code and failure_message fields.
Tip: Add --wait to the create command to block until the video is ready instead of polling manually. The default timeout is 20 minutes — override with --timeout 30m. On timeout, the CLI exits with code 4 and prints the last known resource state along with a hint to resume polling manually.
​
5. Download the Video
Once complete, download to a local file:
heygen video download vid_xyz789 --output-path ./launch-video.mp4

Output
{
  "asset": "video",
  "message": "Downloaded video to ./launch-video.mp4",
  "path": "./launch-video.mp4"
}

If the video was created with captions enabled, you can download the captioned version:
heygen video download vid_xyz789 --asset captioned --output-path ./launch-captioned.mp4

Output Modes

12	## The rules these corrections came from
13

17	
18	- **No em dashes, ever.** [[teaching-voice]], Core voice.
19	- **No internal vocabulary in student copy.** `projects/lsatu/src/features/activity-centre/CONTEXT.md`

27	## Corrections
28	
29	### Contrapositive and arrow lessons: cut the sophistry (2026-10-11)
30	
31	Joshua rejected the first contrapositive lesson as 'terribly done' and then rejected the rewrite for 'a lot of sophistry'. Rules from the two rounds:
32	
33	- **Say what it is first, in plain words, before any example.** Name the idea, say what it states, then show it. The contrapositive is the **same rule said a different way**. The two are **logically equivalent**: if one is true, the other is true, and you can use either. Lead with that. Source: his own teaching, [[contrapositive-teaching]].
34	- **No metaphor-of-obligation language.** Never write that a rule 'makes a promise', that the left side 'depends on' the right, or that it has 'nothing to stand on'. Say what is true and why, in the plainest reasoning: if the left side had happened, the right side would have come with it.
35	- **Keep it natural, intuitive and personable.** Contractions, short sentences, a teacher thinking next to the student. No lecture-hall phrasing. If a sentence sounds like it is trying to sound wise, cut it.
36	- **Name the lesson by its topic.** 'Contrapositive', not 'Write and read the contrapositive'.
37	- **A lesson explains before it drills.** Order for a concept lesson: what it is, see it work, how to do it, what it is not, the steps in short, why it matters. Do not open with an example or a question.
38	- **Must arrow note must say:** what the arrow says, where to start reading (the left), and what the arrow is for. Not just which side is sufficient.
39	- **A card front asks the question, the back gives the answer first.** The answer is the large line. Be specific: 'Can you read a rule from right to left?', not 'Can you reverse the arrow?'. Never 'the other one'.
40	- **Fix the vague word, not the sentence.** 'Reverse' and 'the other one' were both rejected as not specific. Say what the student actually does or what the condition actually is.
41	- **Never call the contrapositive 'the one other thing' a rule tells you.** Joshua (2026-10-11): it is not the only other thing you know. Say the two are equivalent and interchangeable: know one, you know the other. Do not claim a closed list of what else is known.
42	- **Do not use the raffle (win the raffle, get a bicycle) as the example in new contrapositive or arrow material.** Pick a fresh everyday example for each lesson. The contrapositive note now uses an elevator: if it is moving, its doors are closed. The older course text still uses the raffle; replace it as those lessons are revisited.
43	- **Contrapositive lesson (Joshua, 2026-10-11, final): no thought experiment, no guess box, no flip card.** An earlier coat-and-guess card confused him ('I don't know what you did about that coat example'). Follow the source HTML's explanation of the contrapositive directly, in close language, improving only where it can be made clearer: recap the rule, define it up front (if the necessary condition is absent, the sufficient condition is absent too), one first-person example, 'found within the original, waiting to be recognized', logically equivalent and interchangeable, four-situations table, write it with the squiggle and flip and negate, what it is not, read it don't always write it, why it matters. Do not use the source's own examples (coffee, breathing/air). Current example: ceiling light and power.
44	- **Say 'left side of the arrow' and 'right side of the arrow'** in explanations of the arrow and the contrapositive.
45	- **Never mention Logic Games.** Only LR and RC exist.
46	- **Source cautions are good, keep them:** do not say the rule and its contrapositive are 'all you know', and do not say to 'ignore the conditional'. Say the rule settles nothing in the other two situations.
47	- **Say 'must', not just 'is', in a rule's necessary side (Joshua, 2026-10-11):** 'If the ceiling light is on, then the power MUST be on.' The must is the point of the arrow.
48	- **'Foreign' is an awkward word** ('it isn't some foreign process'). Use plain, personable phrasing in his Intro to LR voice: a plain claim, a contrast, short stacked landings ('No power, no light.'), 'Here's the thing', direct 'you', contractions, dry wit.
49	- **Emphasis may be a little louder in the contrapositive lesson:** a slightly cheeky exclamation on the key word ('They're interchangeable!') with a one-time punch-in and glow, plus 'Can't happen!' on the forbidden case. Main Conclusion copy has no exclamation marks in teaching text, so keep them to one or two per lesson.
50	- **Bring lessons to life, but let motion carry the reasoning:** pulse, glow, a punch on the key word, sides that swap and a 'not' that pops on, toggles that show the four situations. Every animation needs a still, readable equivalent and must stop under reduced motion. Existing pieces to reuse: DrillDemo, HypotheticalFigure (data-in-view), SufficientConditionExperience, indicator-word-deck flip card.
51	- **Never show negation as a squiggle (~) beside an idea (Joshua, 2026-10-11, 'never').** Negation is either the word 'not' or a strikethrough line drawn through the idea itself. Applies to every lesson, diagram, drill and animation. Fixed so far: the contrapositive note and its flip-and-negate animation. Still using ~: reference sequence diagrams, sign patterns, worksheet drills and symbol answers (see the next fix pass).
52	- **Negation line runs diagonally from the idea's top left corner to its bottom right corner (Joshua, 2026-10-11).** Use it in written lines and tables as well as animations, not only boxes. Also write the contrapositive out as a plain-English sentence early in the explanation.
53	- **No squiggle anywhere, answer choices included (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** He still saw ~ in the multiple-choice options and the reference practice below the lesson. Every ~ in a string is rendered through the Notation component so it draws as the struck idea; a test now fails if ~ appears on the contrapositive page. Check practice buttons and answer keys, not only lesson prose.
54	- **Wrong must look wrong (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** Reverse and inverse are shown as red cards with an X badge and the word "Wrong", beside one green "Right" card for the contrapositive. Do not put wrong and right in the same plain table. Also: no repeated content on a page (the light and power sentences were said three times), and visuals must look sleek and high end.
55	- **Questions must be unmistakable; no pale green; no broken sentences; every line earns its place (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** The "what the rule tells you" interactive did not make clear what it was asking. Now: a navy panel, a "Your turn" tag, the rule, and one big question ("Which ONE of these four worlds can't happen?"), with four tappable worlds. Colour carries meaning (amber = sufficient idea, teal = necessary idea, and the colour travels with the idea when sides flip). Sentences are never split with line breaks or orphaned (balanced wrapping, one card per sentence). "Logically equivalent" pulses. Cut anything that restates what the page already said (the 4-row table, the raffle recap, the second plain-English line, the repeated light-and-power blockquote). End the lesson with checks that test use of the idea, including one that tests the reverse trap.
56	- **Interactive steps say what to click (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** Label them "Step 1. Click here to ..." and "Step 2. Click here to ...", not "Next step" / "Play it again". The student drives; nothing autoplays. The next step to click glows to invite the click.
57	- **Show the rule, do not just diagram it (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** The original and contrapositive are two tabs on one stage with a real light bulb and switches. Original tab: flip the light switch on and the power node is forced on. Contrapositive tab: cut the power and the bulb is blocked; "Try the light switch anyway" does nothing. Concept pairs get something to operate, not a static pair of boxes.
58	- **Simple beats clever (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** The first light bulb stage had a diagram row, sentence, two icon boxes, a wire, state chips, a switch, a bonus button and a message. He called it too complicated and messy. Rule: one idea per interactive, one control, one sentence. The tab itself carries the diagram; two plain lamps show the state; no wires, chips or extra buttons.
59	- **Raffle retired from the contrapositive page (2026-10-12).** The "Try the four patterns" practice used the raffle. It now has four fresh rules, one per place a "not" can sit (guest list and wristband, shop and shutters, gate and dog, stamped ticket), with reasoned feedback that names the reverse and the inverse. The raffle and bicycle still run through the earlier reference steps, the opening lessons, the media and the shared foundations source (about 300 mentions); those need their own pass and the generated bundle rebuilt.
60	- **Never say "patterns" or "move" to students (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** Both read as awkward jargon. Say "four rules", "try it on", "change", "flip the sides". Practice questions get the same visual quality as the rest of the page: one rule at a time on the navy panel, colour-coded idea pills, large answer tiles, instant reasoned feedback, progress dots. The weakest-looking part of a page gets rebuilt, not patched.
61	- **Words:** 'Correct', not 'Right', on a correct answer. Bold the word 'if' where the point is that the rule says what follows *if* something is true.

63	Still to reconcile (found 2026-10-11, not Joshua decisions): 'establish' is banned below but his approved necessary copy uses it; the raffle was approved for the opening and later rejected as the sufficient anchor; see `.claude/skills/bootcamp-builder/references/teaching-preferences.md`.
64	
65	### Conditional opening: define the words before using them (2026-10-03)
66	
67	Joshua's marked browser paragraphs used “direction” and “guarantee” before making their meaning clear. The purpose paragraph also described an argument accepting more than its information supports without showing what that meant. Teach what a rule lets you conclude in plain language, then attach the vocabulary to a concrete relationship. Explain sufficient as enough and necessary as required before relying on those roles. Show what the must-arrow records and why English clause order does not determine the two sides.
68	
69	2026-10-03 clarification: explicitly distinguish the indicator from the complete condition it refers to. Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. The indicator itself is not a condition in the diagram. Show the same relationship with if at the beginning and in the middle, and show necessary targets both before and after requirement language. Neither sentence position nor the words immediately following the indicator determine placement. Preserve if versus only if as different complete expressions.
70	
71	A rule describes what would follow **if** a condition were true; it does not say that condition happened. Bold that “if” and use meaningful in-view motion to expose the connection, with a static reduced-motion equivalent. Replace compressed ready-order wording with a natural full sentence. Give each paragraph and interaction a specific teaching job. These corrections supplement the canonical voice standard rather than adding a new writing register.
72	
73	### Generic reasoning prompts → concrete numbered demonstrations (2026-09-30)
74	
75	Joshua marked four parts of the V6 opening: "Read each whole idea," "Find what the statements guarantee," the abstract first sentence about information, and the paragraph combining application and linking. They named goals without showing a useful action or explanation. Replace them with numbered, separate scenarios. Show the fact and rule that prove a conclusion about one case; separately show two matching rules and their new conditional conclusion. Labels should identify actual inputs or outputs. Review the whole note for repeated umbrella instructions and make task instructions executable. This follows the teaching voice: specific, concrete, short sentences, with air between distinct ideas.
76	
77	### Fact and rule definitions must explain the inference (2026-09-30)
78	
79	Joshua rejected definitions that remained abstract and an explanation that did not clearly distinguish applying a rule from linking rules. Define the inputs with examples, then show the matching step and exact output. A case fact plus a rule proves something about that case. Two connecting rules produce another conditional rule without establishing a case exists. Adding a fact to the chain is a separate step. Explain all four fact situations and a hidden chain revealed by contraposition rather than leaving them implicit in an interactive model. Rules include general logical relationships, not only policies or obligations.
80	
81	### Boxed `L -> R` → bold teaching notation **L → R** (2026-09-30)
82	
83	Joshua rejected the recap's small monospace arrow inside a pale code box. It looked like implementation text and weakened the explanation. Use a real, clearly weighted arrow in ordinary teaching typography. Explain its complete conditions before shortening them. The Some, Most, and All reference also governs the progression: introduce the whole idea, develop the individual components, then compare and combine them. A visual refresh must review that sequence, not only colors and boxes.
84	
85	### "Concept lesson" → the actual reasoning title (2026-09-30)
86	
87	The recap preview exposed an internal label above the student lesson. Joshua explicitly rejected it. Use a title such as "Facts, Rules, and What Follows" and headings that teach the reasoning. Keep production labels, version metadata, and design commentary in private records. Check the rendered HTML, since its shell can introduce wording absent from the Markdown source.

89	### "Recall Rephrase Colour" → "Read, Recall, Colour"
90	The door's heading was hardcoded to a name the product does not have, one line
91	under a chip reading "Read, Recall, Colour". "Rephrase" belongs to a different
92	Activity, Conditional Rephrase. A test asserted the wrong name, which is why
93	2,600 passing tests never caught it.
94	**Lesson:** when a test pins a string, check the string is right, not just that
95	it matches.
96	
97	### "Intake only. Nothing comes back out yet…" → plain instruction
98	Clinical and abstract, and the sentence then told her to read "with full
99	intonation" while step one is silent reading. The clause that made intonation
100	internal, "hear it naturally in your head", had been dropped in an earlier edit.
101	**Lesson:** a word like "intake" reads as jargon. And when copy is trimmed,
102	check what the trimmed clause was load-bearing for.
103	
104	### "Three beats through the stimulus" → "Three steps"
105	"Beat" is the internal code name for one of the four method steps. The glossary
106	says students see **step**. The interface ten pixels below labelled the same four
107	things Step 1 to Step 4, so the page used two names for one thing.
108	**Lesson:** never let an implementation name reach the page.
109	
110	### "22 pieces to read" → line removed
111	A **piece** is one chunk, roughly a sentence, and the instructions on that same
112	page use the word that way. The count was of stimuli, so "22 pieces" read as 22
113	sentences.
114	**Lesson:** a unit word must mean one thing per surface. Check what a word
115	already means on the page before reusing it.
116	
117	### "say it back" → "tell it back", "The Read" → "The Recall"
118	The library door still carried both retired names. "The Read" was step four's
119	former name and collided with step one, which is why it was renamed.
120	**Lesson:** a rename is not finished until every row carries it. The door was
121	stale for months because it is a different database row from the sections.
122	
123	### "then a fourth" → "then a fourth step"
124	The noun sat three words back and the reader had to carry it.
125	
126	### "once you have got all of it" → "once you have read all of it"
127	Ambiguous between having read it and having remembered it, in the one clause
128	that triggers step four.
129	**Lesson:** precision matters most where the sentence is an instruction.
130	
131	### The three-fact notice → one fact
132	"Nothing is recording yet, the microphone opens on Start, and the section
133	records in one take that you cannot pause." Two of those are said again in step
134	four and in the long version, so she skipped the paragraph.
135	**Lesson:** say it once, in the place it matters.
136	
137	### The method, told twice → the long version collapsed
138	The page rendered four numbered steps and then the whole written briefing saying
139	the same thing at length, both open. She read the method twice and could not
140	tell which one to follow.
141	**Lesson:** two explanations of one thing is worse than either alone.
142	
143	### "PT105.S1 · 24 stimuli · about 25 min" → removed
144	The section was already the second line of the title. The clock is at the foot
145	and in the long version. The stimulus count measured the work in a unit she has
146	no feel for.
147	**Lesson:** delete a line that repeats what is above it. Do not reword it.

149	## Booking emails: hand over the link, not the pass settings (2026-09-08)
150	
151	**Cut:** "70 minutes each, and no expiration date, so there's no rush to use them."
152	**Keep:** "You're set up on LSAT U with 10 sessions."
153	
154	Session length and expiry are admin fields. The student sees them on the booking
155	page when she picks a time, so putting them in the email is detail she did not ask
156	for and has to read past to reach the link. The credit count is worth saying because
157	it tells her how much she has. Everything else about the pass stays in the backend.
158	
159	**Lesson:** in a booking email, say who it's for, how many sessions, and the link.
160	Stop there.

162	## Drill hub: say what the button does, not what the feature is called (2026-09-09)
163	
164	Six changes on `/classroom/drill`, from the run-3 pass. The hub named its own
165	features, and it assumed the student had rejected something.
166	
167	| Was | Now |
168	|---|---|
169	| "Start Smart Drill" | "Practice your weakest types" |
170	| "Start One Passage" | "Read one passage" |
171	| "Start a Smart Drill instead" | "Or practice your weakest types" |
172	| "Read one passage instead" | "Or read one passage now" |
173	| "Open analytics" | "See where you lose points" |
174	| "Open journal" | "Review your missed questions" |
175	
176	"Smart Drill" is the product's internal name for the adaptive set. On a button it
177	asks the student to guess what she is about to get. The sentence above the button
178	still names it, which is where a product name belongs: after the thing has been
179	explained, not as the explanation.
180	
181	"Instead" was the worse one. It tells the student she has just declined the
182	assigned work by reading past it. She has not. "Or" offers a second path without
183	narrating a decision she never made.
184	
185	"Open analytics" and "Open journal" name a screen. A student does not want
186	analytics, she wants to know where the points are going.
187	
188	The fallback paragraph lost "an adaptive set that follows your weakest question
189	types" for "the question types you miss most": the same fact, without the product
190	vocabulary.
191	
192	**Lesson:** a button says what happens next, in the student's words. Feature names
193	belong in the sentence that explains them, never on the control.
194	
195	## Words never to use in student copy
196	
197	From the glossary. Internal names may stay in code, never on the page.
198	
199	| Never | Use instead |
200	|---|---|
201	| beat, beats | step |
202	| chunk | piece |
203	| intake | plain instruction |
204	| briefing, phase, tab, card | the thing's own name |
205	| say it back | tell it back |
206	| The Read | The Recall |
207	| Recall Rephrase Colour | Read, Recall, Colour |
208	| em dash, en dash | a full stop, or a colon before a list |
209	| expression ("Which expression identifies...") | "indicators" for the taught words, "phrase" for a multi-word indicator read as one unit ("read only if as one phrase"), "idea" or "condition" for what it points to. Added 2026-10-11: "expression" is vague and stiff; the student is looking for an indicator, so name it and reinforce the term being taught |
210	| statement ("Statement 1 of 7") | sentence. Added 2026-10-11: students read sentences; "statement" sounds like a logic textbook |
211	| Practise / "N statements" on drill cards | say what the drill has the student do. Added 2026-10-11 |
212	| establish ("enough to establish") | prove, or tells you. Added 2026-10-11: plainer; keep "guarantee", it is plain and sticks |
213	| preserve ("preserves the rule") | keeps. Added 2026-10-11 |
214	| relationship (when a rule is meant) | rule. Added 2026-10-11 |
215	| stipulated | this rule. Added 2026-10-11 |
216	| classify | sort. Added 2026-10-11 |
217	| "That keeps the meaning." on every correct step | name what was right: "Right indicator.", "Right direction."; wrong: "Not quite. Try again." Added 2026-10-11 |
218	| Personalized If/Then | Put yourself in it. Added 2026-10-11: names what the student does, not a feature |
219	| target ("the sufficient target") | what it points to, or the group. Added 2026-10-11; "target" stays in code |
220	| the model ("compare with the model") | our version. Added 2026-10-11 |
221	| "has to be true for it" (legend) | "required for the result". Added 2026-10-11: "it" had nothing to refer to; every label must make sense on its own |
222	| wrong-answer feedback that explains the right answer | a hint about why the chosen answer fails ("“only if” introduces what is required, not what is enough"). Added 2026-10-11: the explanation gave the answer away |
223	| "Build the arrow" | "Build the diagram". Added 2026-10-11: the student builds a diagram; the arrow is one symbol in it |
224	| "Select the words that signal the rule" | "Select the conditional indicator." Added 2026-10-11: name the thing the student is looking for |
225	| "28 sentences", "Sentence 1 of 28", "Next sentence" | questions: "28 questions", "Question 1 of 28", "Next question". Added 2026-10-11: a sentence is not the unit of a drill and reads unprofessional |
226	| "Drill 1" as a title | the task itself, e.g. "Spot the condition". Added 2026-10-11: a number says nothing about the reasoning |
227	| "a rule makes a promise", "depends on", "nothing to stand on", "the other one" | say what is true and why, in plain words. Added 2026-10-11: reads as sophistry; vague words like "the other one" point at nothing |
228	| "Right." on a correct answer | "Correct." Added 2026-10-11 |
229	
230	**Before designing any new homework, bootcamp, drill or note, read this table.** When a
231	word is rejected in review, add it here first, with the reason, then fix the copy.
232	
233	## Conditional Rephrase: name the student action, not the internal model (2026-09-15)
234	
235	**Cut:** “record the route,” “choose the route,” and a map presented as five rules.
236	
237	**Keep:** diagram the statement on paper, choose the matching diagram, and organize
238	the map into four groups. “Required means right” is a separate takeaway because it
239	explains placement; it is not a fifth group.
240	
241	“Route” described the system’s representation, not the student’s work. Calling five
242	unlike ideas rules also flattened the teaching structure and made the layout harder to
243	read.
244	
245	**Lesson:** name the action the student can see herself doing. Do not force a teaching
246	reminder into the same hierarchy as the categories it explains.
247	
248	Related: [[teaching-voice]] · [[ASSIGN-AN-ACTIVITY]] · [[STUDENT-PLATFORM-IDENTITY-MAP]]
249	
250	## Session recaps: write a reusable reference, not a play-by-play (2026-09-12)
251	
252	**Cut:** an opener saying the student got two questions right, followed by a summary of
253	which answer they chose and how they explained it.
254	
255	**Keep:** the concept the student needs tomorrow, a visual model of the reasoning, a
256	short diagnostic check, and the exact practice they need to complete.
257	
258	A student will not remember an isolated answer-choice exchange a day later, and the
259	exchange is not useful unless it demonstrates a reusable rule. A recap should read like
260	a compact study note, not a record of the conversation. Name a question only when the
261	details make the concept easier to apply. Praise belongs only when it identifies a
262	durable skill or meaningful change, not merely that an answer was correct.
263	
264	**Lesson:** organize the recap around what the student can use next. Lead with the
265	concept, show the logic clearly, and finish with tangible practice.
266	
267	### A rule is not a fact (2026-09-12)
268	
269	**Cut:** describing a conditional Must Be True lesson as “applying a rule to a fact”
270	without checking whether the stimulus supplied a fact at all.
271	
272	**Keep:** a fact is an asserted true case or condition. A rule is a general conditional
273	relationship. It tells you what follows if its trigger is present, but it does not say
274	that the trigger occurred. Some stimuli contain rules only, so the work is to chain
275	them. When a fact is present, apply a matching rule or a valid contrapositive.
276	
277	**Lesson:** label the role of each sentence before explaining the inference. Never
278	invent a fact to make a rule fire.
279	
280	### A post-session note is lecture material, not an email (2026-09-12)
281	
282	**Cut:** a greeting, a dated recap heading, references to a question the student is
283	expected to remember, and a sign-off.
284	
285	**Keep:** a concept title, a clear opening distinction, numbered procedures, spaced
286	examples, and practice that uses the lesson.
287	
288	**Lesson:** the note should remain useful when the session itself is no longer fresh.
289	It must teach from the page instead of relying on remembered context.
290	
291	The concept should not arrive as an abrupt list. Begin with a short explanation of
292	why the distinction matters, show the full method at a glance, and then teach each
293	path in the same order. Use tables for comparisons and numbered headings for a
294	procedure so the student can see the structure before reading the detail.
295	
296	### Define the concept in Joshua's language (2026-09-28)
297	
298	**Cut:** “Some, most, and all tell you how much of a group fits a description.”
299	
300	**Keep:** “Some means at least one. Most means more than half. All means every one,”
301	grounded in a concrete set of ten cookies before drawing conclusions from the words.
302	
303	The cut line is vague and does not sound like Joshua teaching. It makes the reader
304	decode “fits a description” before learning any of the three words. It also risks
305	blurring the difference between a count that is possible and one that is guaranteed.
306	
307	**Lesson:** open with the actual distinction or definition Joshua taught. Use a
308	concrete example to show what each statement proves, then introduce notation and
309	the inferences. Do not write an abstract umbrella sentence merely to announce the
310	topic.
311	
312	
313	### Reference quality does not authorize reference topics (2026-09-30)
314	
315	Joshua requested the Some, Most, and All note as a teaching-flow and visual-quality reference. Quantity examples and exercises do not belong in the separate facts/rules note. Remove that off-topic material; retain the distinction between what a rule says and what a fact establishes. Before future recaps, read the accumulated corrections in the Post Session Recap skill's `references/recap-calibration.md`. Preserve source-driven scope while matching the reference's teaching quality.
316	
317	### Conditional practice: explain the decision instead of naming the operation (2026-10-07)
318	
319	Joshua rejected “same rule, different words,” “equivalent-rule comparison,” “find the requirement, then place it,” “work through the expressions below,” “first choose the complete target,” “read the whole idea,” and “sentence X of Y” when these stood in for a useful explanation or made the drill feel like a collection of labeled sections. “Complete target” and similar terminology should not carry a beginner's instructions without a concrete meaning attached.
320	
321	The revised local copy asks what the learner needs to decide: “Who does the rule say will receive a wristband?”, “What kind of key is needed to open the cabinet?”, and “What kind of pass does entry require?” The lesson demonstrates why before noon, current, registered and with yellow tags matter. Dropping one of those words changes which cases the rule covers. An arrow is checked by imagining its left condition and asking what the sentence then establishes, rather than treating the first English clause as the left side.
322	
323	Joshua also asked for the mixed drill to train a skill and build a habit, with one question visible and a progress bar. The current local response uses five stages with a concrete purpose for each: retain the full conditions, build a diagram, attempt a translation before supplied labels, compare meanings, then transfer to unfamiliar cases. Error feedback distinguishes dropped qualifications, reversed roles, required versus enough and changed meaning. A fresh question checks the repaired distinction; correcting the exposed answer alone does not demonstrate transfer. Written translations are compared by the learner and are not represented as automatically graded.
324	
325	Keep modality in the examples. An electric vehicle that is denied permission disproves the claim that being electric guarantees permission; a vehicle merely staying outside does not. A required action is an obligation, not a fact proving compliance. Confirmation practice for that mistake must test another obligation rather than an unrelated wording comparison.
326	
327	These are demonstrated corrections and the current implementation response, not approval of every exact replacement sentence or proof that the voice matches Joshua identically. The [selected voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) still governs wording, cadence, concrete reasoning and scope. [Current session record](log.md) distinguishes local work from publication.
328	
329	Further steering in the same session: “Keep the complete condition” was itself awkward as a visible heading. Ask who or what the particular rule covers instead. Joshua also rejected the landing page's total question/stage counts, per-stage counts, long stage list and padded explanation of repairs. A shorter launch should explain the immediate task; each stage should introduce its purpose and instructions when the learner reaches it. That latest flow revision was requested while final verification was still pending, so this record does not certify its implementation.
330	
331	
332	### RC actions should describe what the student gets (2026-10-07)
333	
334	Joshua rejected Inspect set as unclear and awkward. Use Preview drill for viewing the exact selected passages and questions before starting. Keep Start this drill distinct and explain whether answers are hidden and whether work is saved. Generic Menu is also insufficient where the actual action is Question details. This correction does not justify adding more launch instructions, repeated summaries, or unrelated assigned work inside RC setup.

121	  return <NoteSection title="Try it on four rules">
122	    <p>A rule can have a “not” nowhere, on the right, on the left or on both sides. Every time: flip the sides, put “not” on both ideas, and take off any “not” that was already there. Try one of each.</p>
